             7.4 UALink Virtual Pods
             A workload may require all the Accelerators in a Pod to meet its compute needs or it may require
             only a subset of the Accelerators. To use the Pod resources efficiently, the Pod Controller may
             permit partitioning the Pod into Virtual Pods. The Virtual Pods are assignable to individual tenants,
             allowing multiple tenants to share the Pod and run their workload in their respective partitions
             concurrently. These Virtual Pods may be restricted to a single Accelerator within a System Node,
             may encompass multiple Accelerators within the same System Node, or may span multiple System
             Nodes (up to all the Accelerators in the Pod) via the Switches. Virtual Pods also require compute
             and network resources (i.e., for use by virtual machines that run on the System Node’s host CPUs).
             An Accelerator shall not belong to more than a single Virtual Pod at a given time. Further,
             Accelerators that are members of a Virtual Pod shall be assigned as a whole; fractional assignment
             of Accelerators is not permitted in a Virtual Pod. If SR-IOV is utilized on an SR-IOV capable
             Accelerator, the SR-IOV Virtual Functions are not isolated from each other with respect to traffic on
             the UALink fabric and therefore must be assigned to the same tenant security domain.

                                                  Manageability Requirements                                   187

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                       Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                           Rev 2.0

             Each System Node participating in a Virtual Pod hosts the tenant VM, and the appropriate
             Accelerators on the System Node are assigned to that VM. The VM hosts the device driver, e.g. VF
Evaluation Copy
             drivers for SR-IOV supported Accelerators, that send control messages to the Accelerator for
             configuring the Accelerator including the UALink ports (VM/VF specific configuration) and
             programming the device for data transfers between VM and Accelerator and between peer
             Accelerators in a Virtual Pod.
             The following figure illustrates a UALink Pod that has been partitioned by the Pod Controller into
             three Virtual Pods. Virtual Pod 1 contains a subset of the Accelerators in System Node 1. Virtual Pod
             2 contains a subset of the Accelerators in both System Nodes 1 and 2. Virtual Pod 3 contains all the
             accelerators in System Node 3. One Accelerator on System Node 2 is not part of any Virtual Pod and
             is excluded from routing tables on the Switches.

                                                                              UALink

                                                                              Controller



                  UALink Pod

                               UALink Switch Platform 1                 UALink Switch Platform 2            UALink Switch Platform 3




                        Acc       Acc      Acc       Acc          Acc        Acc      Acc      Acc        Acc        Acc    Acc        Acc

                              UALink System Node 1                      UALink System Node 2                    UALink System Node 3

                      Virtual Pod 1      Virtual Pod 2                                               Virtual Pod 3

             7.5 Manageability Workflows
             The requirements for managing a UALink Pod are documented in the Ultra Accelerator Link
             Manageability Specification, including workflows for:
                  •      Accelerator and Switch admission to the Pod
                  •      Pod topology discovery and validation
                  •      Switch configuration including partitioning and routing table setup
                  •      Virtual Pod creation
                  •      Link, Accelerator, and Switch failure and recovery
                  •      Error reporting such as drop counters and link state changes
                  •      Accelerator telemetry such as transmit/receive statistics
                  •      Switch telemetry such as port-level statistics and Switch health reporting

             8 Security
Evaluation Copy
             The UALink link protection feature, referred to as UALinkSec, is intended to protect traffic on a
             UALink network from a physically present adversary; the adversary might be present at the time of
             the attack or may have placed a device (e.g., an interposer) to snoop or tamper with the UALink
             traffic. Additionally, in platforms that support Confidential Computing (CC), UALinkSec protects the
             Tenant data on a UALink network from the infrastructure provider and from other Tenants
             potentially collocated on the same UALink cluster. CC implies that a Trusted Execution
             Environment (e.g., Intel TDX, AMD SEV and ARM CCA) exists on the platform; the TEE under the
             control of the Tenant is responsible for UALinkSec configuration. When enabled, UALinkSec
             minimally provides data confidentiality and optionally data integrity (including replay protection)
             to UALink network traffic. The next section provides an example of system used to illustrate
             UALinkSec principles followed by the Security Model in Section 8.3.

             8.1 References
             SPDM: Security Protocol and Data Model (SPDM) over MCTP Binding Specification:
             https://www.dmtf.org/dsp/DSP0275


             AES-GCM: NIST Special Publication 800-38D Recommendation for Block Cipher Modes of
             Operation- Galois/Counter Mode (GCM) and GMAC:
             https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/38/d/final


             TDISP: Refer to PCIe Express specification for TDISP:
             https://pcisig.com/specifications


             Key Derivation Function: NIST SP 800-108 Rev. 1, “Recommendation for Key Derivation Using
             Pseudorandom Functions”, August 2022 (includes updates as of February 2, 2024), URL:
             https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/108/r1/upd1/final


             KMAC256: NIST SP 800-185, “SHA-3 Derived Functions: cSHAKE, KMAC, TupleHash, and
             ParallelHash”, December 2016, URL:
             https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/185/final

             8.2 System Overview
             An UALink Pod is comprised of a set of System Nodes each of which may have one or more
             accelerators. Each accelerator will have one or more UALink ports. The accelerators within the Pod
             communicate with each other via a switch-based UALink fabric. It is assumed that every accelerator
             in a Pod can communicate with every other accelerator in the Pod. The Pod shall be managed by a
             central Pod Controller software, running on platform(s) outside of the Pod, that works in concert
             with Node Management Agents on the System Node. The system software such as host OS and
             hypervisor is responsible for performing actual resource management and scheduling on each
             System Node as directed by the Pod Controller.
                                                           Security                                           189

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                    Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                        Rev 2.0

             The Pod Controller creates a Virtual Pod that contains the number of accelerators needed by the
             tenant and then configures the UALink ports on the accelerators and the UALink switches for
Evaluation Copy
             communication within that Virtual Pod. Each System Node shall run tenant software, such as a
             tenant VM, and the accelerators (including the UALink ports) that are part of tenant’s Virtual Pod
             shall be assigned to the tenant VM, with the actual assignment tasks performed by the OS or
             hypervisor on the System Nodes.
             The tenant VMs on each System Node shall configure the accelerators including programming of
             tenant specific keys. The tenant VMs belonging to different System Nodes may communicate using
             frontside networking regarding configuration and programming of accelerators in the Virtual Pod..
             The tenant that intends to use collectives shall also configure the switches for collective operations
             such as programming the group table in the switch for collective operations. The communication
             between tenant SW on the host and switch SW/FW for group table and other collective
             configuration may occur over UALink via Load/Store operation.
             There are two distinct role-based software execution domains; one is the infrastructure owner
             controlled remote and local management software (and firmware) that manage compute and
             networking resources at Pod, System Node and switch level and the other is the tenant software
             that run on the compute resources assigned to it and use the UALink network to allow its
             accelerators to access each other’s data. A tenant that uses collectives will also have resources
             assigned on the switch for collective operations. The UALink security for confidential computing
             use requires HW enforced isolation of these two execution domains to provide strong security
             assurance to the tenant application in presence of potential SW and HW exploits in the datacenter.
             For more details on the manageability aspects of an UALink Pod including Virtual Pod creation and
             its lifecycle management, refer to the manageability chapter in this specification.

             8.3 Security model
             This section describes the security objectives for the UALink Link Protection feature, the
             corresponding TCB (Trusted Computing Base) and the adversary profile and capabilities.
             One of the use cases for the UALink protection feature is Confidential Computing (CC). Confidential
             Computing mandates the most stringent threat model for the use cases envisioned, where the
             Infrastructure provider is not trusted. As such, the security model presented in this section is
             defined with CC in mind; UALinkSec aims at providing the owner of a Virtual Pod (called the Tenant
             hereafter) control over the security of its data, without reliance on the Infrastructure provider to
             meet the security objectives stated below.

             8.3.1 Security objectives:
             The UALink link protection feature aims at providing confidentiality and optional integrity
             (including replay protection) for data exchanged between accelerators belonging to the same
             Virtual Pod.
             If the tenant uses collectives, the UALink link protection feature also aims to provide confidentiality
             and optional integrity of data exchanged between the accelerator and switch for collective
             operations.
             These security objectives are qualified by the adversary profile and capabilities described in Section
             8.3.3.


                                                             Security                                           190

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

             Note: Availability is not in scope for the UALink link protection feature, and it is considered the
             responsibility of the infrastructure provider.
Evaluation Copy
             8.3.2 Trusted Computing Base (TCB)
             The TCB describes the trusted elements that UALinkSec relies on to meet its security objectives:
                  •   The Tenant is at the heart of the TCB as it deploys TVM (aka Trusted OS domains) that
                      control the accelerators in the Virtual Pod assigned by the Pod controller
                  •   All the accelerators (hardware and firmware) belonging to the Virtual Pod assigned to the
                      Tenant are trusted, after they are brought in TCB. The accelerators in an OS domain are
                      brought in TCB by the TVM through standard secure mechanisms for authentication and
                      attestation (e.g., SPDM, DICE) that are out of scope for the UALinkSec specifications. For
                      instance, if the accelerators are attached through PCIe to the Host CPUs running the TVM,
                      the PCI SIG TDISP standard should be leveraged by the TVM to bring the accelerators in
                      TCB.
                  •   The switches that perform collectives for a tenant are trusted after they are brought into the
                      TCB. The switches are brought into TCB by the TVM through similar standard mechanisms
                      for authentication and attestation as the ones used to bring accelerators into the TCB.
             For clarity, the untrusted agents are captured below. However, the list is not exhaustive, and any
             agents not listed as part of the TCB shall be considered untrusted:
                  •   The hardware and firmware of UALink switch not used for collectives are not trusted and
                      are not brought into tenant’s TCB.
                  •   All control and management firmware and software provided by the infrastructure provider
                      are untrusted, including the hypervisor running on the Host CPU as well as the Pod
                      controller software and firmware running on a BMC or potentially as a service process on
                      Host CPUs.
                           o Note: Our threat model addresses the scenario where a host SW arbitrarily adds or
                              removes a Virtual Pod member with malicious intent.

             8.3.3 Adversary profile and capabilities
             Table 8-1 below describes the types of attack the adversary can attempt to mount an exploit.
                                                    Table 8-1 Possible attack types
               Adversary tools                  Description                                    Adversary goal

                                                any accessible interface to read, analyze      observe and analyze patterns.
                                                UALink traffic
               Transaction corruption           The adversary either replaces genuine          The purpose of the adversary is to get the
                                                packets on UALink link with chosen ones        corrupted packets accepted and
                                                or flip specific bits of packets in transit.   consumed at destination.
               Transaction replay               The adversary records packets and injects      The purpose of the adversary is to get the
                                                them later on.                                 replayed packets accepted and consumed
                                                                                               at destination.
               Transaction deletion             The adversary intercepts and drops a           The purpose of the adversary is to get the
                                                packet sent by a source in order for the       destination to consume stale data, for
                                                destination to never receive it.               instance by preventing a memory
                                                                                               location from being updated.
               Transaction injection            The adversary crafts a packet it injects       The purpose of the adversary is to get the
                                                toward an accelerator of its choice.           injected packets accepted and consumed
                                                                                               at destination.
               Endpoint spoofing                The adversary assumes the identity of a        The purpose of the adversary is to steal
                                                valid endpoint                                 sensitive data by sending read request or
                                                                                               receiving read response.

                                                                  Security                                                              191

                       © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0



             UALinkSec aims at countering two types of adversaries: physically present and remote adversaries.
Evaluation Copy
             The Physically present adversary may be an Infrastructure provider employee, a system debugger or
             an intruder getting physical access to the UALink Network to perform the malicious operations
             specified in Table 8-1 and build a successful exploit. Such an adversary may:
                  •   Perform a live attack while being physically present, e.g., by probing the UALink links
                  •   Place an interposer to snoop or inject packets in live traffic at a later time
                  •   Replace accelerators with vulnerable ones or from malicious manufacturers, untrusted by
                      the Tenant
             The Remote adversary typically leverages firmware or software vulnerabilities in the system to gain
             access to the UALink traffic and perform the malicious operations specified in Table 8-1 and build a
             successful exploit.
             Note: An adversary may be a hybrid of the two profiles above, where a physically present adversary
             (e.g., system debugger) leverages a vulnerable firmware or software to build a successful exploit.

             8.3.4 Security Assumptions
             UALinkSec has been defined with the following security assumptions:
                  •   It is expected that the platform security solution and the accelerator design provide a
                      mechanism for the Tenant to securely configure the UALink endpoint (e.g. configure
                      encryption settings) and program the Tenant specific master key(s). The exact mechanism
                      is out of scope for the UALink specification and is implementation specific.
                  •   The UALink fabric is ordered, i.e., packets are processed cryptographically in the same order
                      at the source and at the destination.
                  •   Packet headers for both request and response shall be encrypted to meet the UALinkSec
                      security objectives, except for the fields required for routing, request/response
                      compression and flow control. When integrity protection is enabled, fields required for
                      routing, request/response compression and flow control are integrity protected but shall
                      remain in plaintext.
                  •   When an accelerator or a switch is accepted by the Tenant as part of its Virtual Pod and
                      UALinkSec is enabled, all the traffic is protected with UALinkSec, i.e., the standard does not
                      support mixing protected and unprotected traffic.
                  •   Key materials shall be adequately protected in the accelerator to minimally meet the threat
                      model defined in this specification. How this is achieved is outside the scope of this
                      specification and is implementation specific.
                  •   The firmware and software ingredients in TCB are free from vulnerabilities and are not
                      exploitable by the adversary.
                  •   The hardware ingredients in TCB are free from defects and their capabilities (e.g., privilege
                      debug capability) cannot be exploited by the adversary.
                  •   When security mode is enabled, it is enabled for all the ports on an accelerator including
                      bifurcated 2 lane ports and 4 lane port.


             8.3.5 Threat model
             Threat model is given in Table 8-2 below:

              UALinkSec secrets (e.g., keys or     accelerators. It is the responsibility of the accelerator manufacturer to properly protect key

              packets between two UALink           have the key, an injected or corrupted packet is detected by failure of the verification of the
              ports                                integrity tag.

                                                   packet will likely result in unexpected behavior as decryption will use a different counter
                                                   value than the one used for encryption of the packet.

              packets                              integrity tag computation is based on a counter (nonce) that is incremented for each packet

             8.4 UALink System Security
             The confidential computing capable UALink system is assumed to have baseline TEE capabilities on
             each system node.
             Following assumptions are made about the UALink system
                  •   A Pod may have a mix of confidential and non-confidential Virtual Pods running
                      concurrently
                  •   An accelerator that is part of a multi-accelerator virtual Pod shall be assigned to a single
                      tenant e.g. single VM if virtualized. An accelerator that is partitioned into multiple virtual
                      functions (i.e., multiple virtual accelerator instances) and assigned to multiple VMs shall not
                      be added to a Virtual Pod and shall not be allowed to access the UALink fabric.
                                                                          Security                                                               193

                       © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

                  •   UALink subsystem shall be disabled and isolated from all other accelerator subsystems if
                      the accelerator is not configured for communicating via UALink fabric. The accelerators
Evaluation Copy
                      that are not configured for scale-up shall not allow access to or from the UALink fabric to
                      prevent network attacks
             The following security features are assumed on the CPU:
                  •   Shall be TEE capable allowing Tenant data and control on the host to be protected in a TVM.
                  •   Shall support TEE-IO to allow bringing TEE capable accelerators into TVM’s TCB. While
                      UALinkSec does not mandate it, it is recommended that CPU has TEE-IO support (for
                      example, AMD SEV-TIO and Intel TDX Connect) for devices that are TEE Device Interface
                      Security Protocol (TDISP) compliant.
                  •   Shall support accelerator authentication and link protection on the link between host and
                      device.
             The accelerator shall be TEE capable with secure interface to the host to enable TEE-IO with TVM.
             The accelerator is assumed to have support for:
                  •   TDISP version 1.2 or above or equivalent security protocol. It shall allow locking the device
                      configuration, providing device report and status and enabling secure MMIO and DMA.
                  •   A unique cryptographic device identifier and device attestation enabling establishment of
                      authenticated secure session between host and the accelerator. It is recommended that the
                      accelerator support DMTF defined SPDM 1.3 or equivalent protocol for device attestation
                      and secure session setup between TEE security manager (TSM) on the host and Device
                      Security Manager (DSM) on the device.
                  •   If virtualization is supported, device shall provide HW enforced isolation of PF and VF.
             The INC capable switch is assumed to be TEE capable and provide a secure interface to the node SW
             for programming the encryption key material and for secure configuration of collective settings.
             Switch is assumed to support:
                  •   A unique cryptographic device identifier
                  •   SPDM 1.3 or equivalent protocol for switch attestation and establishment of secure session
                      with the switch.
                  •   A switch security manager (SSM), responsible for managing the security of the Virtual Pod
                      Partition in the switch. This is equivalent to the TDISP DSM in a PCIe device. The SSM is
                      required to expose a security interface, equivalent to the TDISP interface, to the trusted SW
                      on the host. The trusted SW is expected to use this interface to lock the switch
                      configuration, such as the routing table, associated with the tenant’s Virtual Pod partition, to
                      transition the Virtual Pod Partition to run state and to read Virtual Pod Partition settings. If
                      the switch detects a security error such as an integrity failure in the crytpo engine, the SSM
                      will transition the Virtual Pod Partition to stop state and stop processing any incoming data
                      from Virtual Pod members and stop sending any outgoing messages to the Virtual Pod
                      members.
                  •   Secure storage and management of cryptographic keys used for link encryption and SPDM
                      sessions.
                  •   Isolation of resources associated with a Virtual Pod partition, such as group table, memory,
                      and other assets from local and remote adversaries.
                                                              Security                                            194

                       © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                     Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                         Rev 2.0

             The system level security architecture described below assumes accelerator to be TDISP compliant
             and describes the security workflow using TDISP context. If implementation uses some other device
Evaluation Copy
             security protocol, the system level security architecture shall be adapted accordingly. The choice of
             device security protocol does not affect UALinkSec directly but may affect security workflow such
             as UALink key programming.
             Figure 8-1 shows a system level view of confidential computing in a pod. The components that are
             not in Tenant’s TCB include Pod controllers, Node Management Agents, host OS, PF drivers and
             hypervisors. These perform resource management, allocation and scheduling as they would for a
             non-confidential computing use case. The TCB includes TVM, TSM and the accelerator, including the
             UALink ports. The figure shows switches to be outside the TCB, however, they are part of the TCB if
             the tenant workload performs collective operations.
             The TVM shall verify the attestation of Virtual Pod’s compute elements and verify the correctness of
             Virtual Pod configuration before running its application. This may be done by a Confidential Cluster
             Manager (CCM) within the TVM as shown in Figure 8-1.



                                                                                   UALink
                                                                                   Pod
                                                                                   Controller



                  UALink Pod
                       Switch                                      Switch                                       Switch
                     Mgmt Agent   UALink Switch Platform 1       Mgmt Agent    UALink Switch Platform 2       Mgmt Agent   UALink Switch Platform 3




                     Acc          Acc      Acc       Acc            Acc           Acc       Acc      Acc            Acc        Acc       Acc      Acc
                                           DSM      DSM            DSM           DSM        DSM
                            UALink System Node 1                              UALink System Node 2                         UALink System Node 3
                                              Virtual Pod

                                             Trusted VM                Trusted VM
                                   T                                                         T
                     Node Mgmt           CCM     Application      CCM         Application
                       Agent       S                                                         S    Node Mgmt                                    Node Mgmt
                                                                                                    Agent                                        Agent
                                   M          Acc Driver               Acc Driver            M
                                         Kernel                   Kernel



                           TCB of Virtual Pod
                                                 Figure 8-1 System level view of confidential computing in a pod

             A Tenant running a Virtual Pod spanning several System nodes shall have a TVM on each of its
             System nodes. The Pod Controller shall provide a list of tenant IP addresses for all TVMs to every
             TVM in the Virtual Pod which allows the TVMs to attest each other and establish cryptographically
             protected sessions with each other. All communication between TVMs shall occur via the front-end
             network and shall be encrypted and integrity protected.

                                                                                   Security                                                                195

                        © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                   Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                       Rev 2.0

             When creating a confidential computing Virtual Pod, the initial steps for creating a Virtual Pod
             maybe similar to a non-CC Virtual Pod creation. TVMs shall perform a set of security steps to verify
Evaluation Copy
             attestation of all devices in the TCB and verify the correct construction and configuration of the
             Virtual Pod. If verification succeeds, TVM shall lock the configuration of the accelerators (including
             the UALink port configurations) and program UALink keys in the UALink ports before starting the
             workload. A lead TVM, typically the one running on the System node where the workload was first
             initiated, is expected to gather the security configuration of all accelerator members via the TVMs
             on other nodes and verify that they have been configured correctly as per Tenant’s security policy.
             An important check includes ensuring all accelerators in the Virtual Pod have unique identifiers.
             The lead TVM shall then generate the UALink master keys and distribute it to other TVMs over
             secure channel. All TVMs shall then program the keys into the UALink ports in the local accelerators
             via a secure interface.
             The secure key programming interface shall provide confidentiality, integrity and replay protection
             of the keys in accordance with the security model described earlier. The secure key programming
             interface may be in the form of device specific interface exposed to the VF driver allowing TVM to
             program the keys through secure memory mapped interface in the virtual function hardware.
             Alternately, the keys may be delivered from TVM to the UALink port using SPDM messages between
             TSM and DSM. These are implementation choices as long as the security requirements are met.
             The accelerators shall be assigned accelerator identifiers by the Pod Controller when the pod comes
             up and is configured. This accelerator identifier serves as the identity of the transmitter and
             receiver in the UALink transactions and shall be unique across the virtual pod. Since Pod controller
             is outside the TCB and is not trusted to ensure uniqueness of accelerator identifiers, when a Virtual
             Pod is created, the lead TVM is responsible for getting accelerator identifiers of all accelerators in
             the Virtual Pod from all TVMs in the Virtual Pod and ensuring they are unique.
             If the workload uses collectives, similar steps are taken to securely configure the accelerators and
             switches for accelerator-switch link protection as described in Section 8.6.




                                                            Security                                           196

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

             8.5 Encryption and authentication scheme for UALink

             To meet the security objectives, UALink standard uses AES-GCM. AES-GCM data processing is
             performed in the protocol/functional layer as shown in Figure 8-2 below:
                                        AES-GCM with 256

                                       Figure 8-2 Encryption and Authentication touch points in UALink stack

             AES-GCM for UALink will have the following parameters:
                  A. Key size - 256 Bit
                  B. Tag size - 8Bytes or 0B (i.e., Tag is optional).
             Encryption is done on a per UALink transaction basis. The encrypted transaction along with the
             optional tag is sent over the UPLI interface to the transaction layer. The transaction layer shall send
             the transaction along with the tag by creating a tag half flit that accompanies the control half flit as
             illustrated in the example flit format in Figure 8-3 below:

             The order of the tags in the tag half flit shall be exactly same as the order of the requests/responses
             in control half flit. Note that the tag is for request + payload or response + payload in case the
             request or response has an accompanying payload.

             8.5.1 Control Half-Flit field encryption and authentication
             UALink Control Half-Flit fields are only partially encrypted as certain sub-fields are necessary for
             routing and flow control. The only sub-fields that are encrypted are Address [17:2] , UPLI
             transaction identifier Tag and ASI in requests. The only sub-field that is encrypted in responses is
             the UPLI transaction identifier Tag.
             When the integrity mode is enabled, select Control Half-Flit sub-fields are integrity protected.
             Request channel signals that are encrypted and authenticated are given in Table 8-3.

             8.5.1.1 Source ID Lookup for Response
Evaluation Copy
             The transmitter shall maintain a lookup table of outstanding Requests and the source ID of
             destination accelerators the Request was sent to. Using the RspTag, which contains the ReqTag field
             from the initial Request, the transmitter shall determine the source ID of the responder. This
             lookup function is not part of TL. It may be implemented in the crypto engine or the accelerator
             functional layer.

             8.5.2 Data authentication and encryption
             All bytes of data and associated Byte Enables (if any) are encrypted and (optionally) integrity
             protected for writes, atomic requests and read responses as described in Table 8-4 through Table
             8-6.

             8.5.3 ISOLATE response handling
             Decryption engine shall ignore ISOLATE response – ISOLATE response shall not be decrypted or
             integrity checked.

             8.5.4 Modes of operation
             An implementation can choose to support:
                  1. No security features
                  2. Encryption only
                  3. Encryption and integrity
             In case an implementation has encryption only, then the following modes shall be supported:
                  1. Encryption enabled
                  2. Encryption disabled
             In case an implementation has encryption and integrity, then the following modes shall be
             supported:
                  1. Encryption enabled
                  2. Encryption and integrity enabled
                  3. Encryption and integrity disabled

             8.5.5 Poisoned data handling with security enabled
             Encryption engine will skip the data beat and Byte Enable that has poison indication set while doing
             encryption and authentication tag generation. The encryption engine shall set all bytes of the
             poisoned beat to 0 and Byte enable to 0 before sending it on UPLI interface. The poison indicator
             shall be passed along with each data beat to the transmit path of the TL. The TL shall replace each
             poisoned data beat with 2 32B “poison” messages.
             On the receive path, TL will decode the poison messages and assert the poison indicator for the
             corresponding 64B data beat on the UPLI interface. TL shall ensure that all bytes of the 64B
             poisoned data beat is 0 and Byte enable is 0 on UPLI interface. The decryption engine will skip
             poisoned data bytes and Byte Enable during the decryption and authentication tag check process.




                                                           Security                                          206

                      © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

             8.5.6 Ordering requirements imposed due to AES-GCM
             AES-GCM requires the sender and receiver to keep the varying part of the IV (essentially the 32-bit
Evaluation Copy
             Invocation field) in lockstep with each other.
             To achieve lockstep operation of sender (encryption) and receiver side (decryption), UALink
             transactions are grouped into three independent streams with transactions belonging to the same
             stream being kept strictly in order while travelling from a given source port to a given destination
             port through the UALink fabric.
             The streams are defined as follows:
                      A. Request stream (Stream Index 0)– this stream has Read requests, Write requests +
                         associated payload and Atomic requests + associated payload.
                      B. Read response stream (Stream Index 1) – this stream has Read responses + associated
                         payload and atomic requests + associated payload.
                      C. Write response stream (Stream Index 2) – this stream has write responses
             These streams are defined on a per VC basis where each VC has 3 streams.
             8.5.6.1 Notes on ordering
                  •   There are no ordering requirements between different streams.
                  •   There are no ordering requirements between traffic from different source ports to a given
                      destination port.
                  •   There are no ordering requirements between traffic from a given source port to different
                      destination ports.
                  •   There are no ordering requirements between traffic belonging to different VCs when per VC
                      state replication is present.

             8.5.7 Authentication and Encryption/Decryption Implementation in an UALink

             8.5.7.1.1 Master key management
             As mentioned previously, an UALink TX has an active master key and an alternate master key.
             Similarly, an UALink RX has an active master key and an alternate master key. These keys are
             configured by secure SW/FW. UAlink RX and TX are required to offer a secure interface to SW/FW
             for programming the two master keys. At any instant, one of the two programmed keys is the
             “active” key and the other is the “alternate” key. Determination of whether a particular key is the
             active key or the alternate key in UALink TX/RX is determined in the following way:
                  •   When the two keys are programmed in for the very first time and secure transmission
                      enabled, hardware selects the first key as the “active” key.
                  •   Later, when a key swap flow is triggered by SW, HW promotes the alternative key to
                      become the active key. When this happens, the original active key is marked as stale by
                      hardware and software is required to program a new key replacing the stale key.
             Hardware shall have mechanisms to ensure that a stale key is never selected as an active key. If the
             key swap flow is triggered and the alternate key is marked as stale, the hardware shall stop
             processing new transactions for that destination/from that source and report an error event to the
             security processor in the accelerator. The security processor upon receiving this error event, shall
             report to the CCM(TVM) that manages the accelerator with information about the Port number that
             reported the event.
                  •   Hardware shall provide mechanisms to inform SW that a key has become stale. HW reports
                      the stale key once the key swap flow is complete for all destinations (TX).
                  •    Hardware shall provide mechanisms for SW to replace a stale key with a fresh key.

             8.5.7.2 Key Derivation Function
             To ensure UALinkSec provides strong cryptographic protection, the derivation of key material used
             by the AES-GCM-256 encryption shall be performed using a strong and NIST approved Key
             Derivation Function (KDF).
             The key derivation function “KDF-KMAC256”, or shortly “KDF” for UALinkSec, follows the
             definitions “KDF using KMAC” provided in Section 4.4 of NIST SP 800-108 Rev. 1 and “KMAC256”
             algorithm provided in Section 4 of NIST SP 800-185.
             The KDF-KMAC256 (K, X) function shown in Figure 8-6 is defined as:
                  KMAC256 (K, X, L = 256, S = “KDF”)
             The input parameters for KDF-KMAC256 are:
                  1.   K: 256-bit key-derivation key
                  2.   X: 88-bit context value
                  3.   L: is fixed to 256 so that KDF outputs a 256-bit key value
                  4.   S: is “KDF” in 8-bit ASCII characters as suggested in Section 4.4 of NIST SP 800-108 Rev. 1.




                                                Figure 8-6 KDF-KMAC256 (K, X) function

             The 256-bit key derivation key K is programmed into the accelerator as one of the active and
             alternate master key by software as described in the Master Key Management section.

             8.5.7.3 Initialization Vector (IV) Construction for AES-GCM
             UALink uses 96-bit Initialization Vector (IV) for AES-GCM. By following Section 8.2.1 of NIST SP
             800-38D, the IV is constructed in a deterministic way so that it is the concatenation of 64-bit fixed
             field and the 32-bit invocation field
             The IV construction is different for Accelerator to switch link protection which is described in
             Section 8.6.
             The 96-bit IV is later concatenated with 32-bit block counter values and provided as 128-bit input
             to the AES engines in AES-GCM. In UALink accelerators, the block counter is reset to 0 for each new
             transaction.
             8.5.7.3.1 IV construction for Accl-Accl link protection
             For an accelerator-accelerator pair, the 64-bit fixed field contains the source & destination
             accelerator IDs, source accelerator port number, as well as the stream ID and VC ID between the
             accelerators. The 32-bit invocation field is defined as an integer counter incremented with each
             transaction from source accelerator to destination accelerator. The detailed IV fields at TX and RX
             are provided in Table 8-8.

             Whether virtual channel (VC) support is enabled or not, the 64-bit fixed field always includes the 2-
             bit VC ID as one of the fields. It is set to 0 when VC support is not enabled, or to the actual value of
             VC ID otherwise. Please refer to the Section 8.5.12.1 for the virtual channel support.
             The integer counter in the invocation field is reset to 0 when a new AES-GCM key is derived. The
             maximum allowable number of AES-GCM invocations per derived key is restricted to 2^32-1,
             thereby ensuring that the same IV value is not reused with the same key.




             8.5.7.3.3 Key identification at Transmitter port and receiver port
             For a 2.0 version compliant transmitter port, the derived key to use for transmitting a request or
             response is determined by the destination accelerator ID.
             For a 2.0 version compliant receiver port, the derived key to use a received request is determined
             by the source accelerator ID.
             For a 2.0 version compliant receiver port, the derived key to use for receiving a response is
             determined by the response tag.


             8.5.7.4 TX implementation
             As mentioned in Section 8.5, in an UALink port TX, there shall be an active master key and an
             alternate master key. The active key is fed into the KDF to generate a derived key. This key
             derivation is detailed in Section 8.5.7.2. The derived key is then used along with a destination and
             stream specific 96-bit IV defined earlier to encrypt transactions belonging to each stream. A derived
             key usage counter tracks the number of transactions encrypted using the current derived key. If the
             derived key usage counter becomes equal to a software configured “derived key expiry” threshold
             and that destination’s “Epoch” counter is incremented. The key derivation flow for TX is detailed in
             Section8.5.7.4.1. The key derivation flow uses the “KeyDeriveMSG.ReqChannel” ,
             “KeyDeriveMSG.RdRspChannel” and “KeyDeriveMSGWrRspChannel” Requests.
             If the master key usage counter values reach a software configured “Master key expiry” threshold
             and a valid active key is not available, then TX hardware shall stop encryption of new transactions
             and shall assert an error event to the security processor of the accelerator. The IV counter shall be 0
             for the very first transaction that a TX stream encrypts using a new derived key.
             8.5.7.4.1 TX Stream-Key derivation flow
             The key derivation flow is done on a per TX, RX pair basis and is managed by the TX port. The key
             derivation flow can be triggered by SW during initialization by writing to a configuration register
             bit or by HW (whenever the derived key expiry threshold is hit) – the key derivation flow will take

             current active master key as input to KDF and derive a new key. If a new derived key is required
             and the active key is not valid, then an error is signaled and all further processing of traffic to the
Evaluation Copy
             destination accelerator is stopped. The key derivation flow involves co-ordination between TX and
             remote RX ports. This TX-RX handshake is illustrated in Figure 8-10

Evaluation Copy
             Note: As per NIST recommendations, the maximum number of usages of a specific derived key
             value is limited to 2^32-1.
             Note: Programmers should ensure that the threshold is not programmed with too low a value –
             otherwise, key rolls and key derivations might be unsustainably frequent.
             Note: programmers must ensure that the threshold value provides sufficient time to complete the
             key derivation handshake before the hard limit of 2^32-1 transactions is hit.
             Note: if a TX or RX times out on the KeyDeriveMSG requests it sends as part of the key derivation
             flow for a particular destination accelerator, then it will report an error to the security processor
             and stop processing of traffic to that destination accelerator and discard such traffic.
             Note: For ease of implementation, it is expected that a Transaction tag value is reserved for use by
             the security engine for key derivation/key swap requests.
             8.5.7.4.2 TX Master key swap flow
             The master key swap flow is triggered by SW. The key swap flow is done by TX for each destination
             accelerator.
             When SW triggers a key swap flow, TX will take the following actions:
                         1. TX will set a key swap message timeout timer.
                         2. If TX hits key derivation threshold for a particular destination, TX will set the
                            “switch to alternate master key” bit in the KeyDeriveMSG.ReqChannel that it sends
                            to that destination. TX will track which all destinations have been sent the “switch to
                            alternate master key”.
                         3. Once the key swap message timer expires, TX will check if there are any destinations
                            for whom the “switch to alternate master key” was not sent. TX shall trigger a key
                            derivation flow for all such destinations such that all destinations gets the “switch to
                            alternate master key” indication.
                         4. Once a particular destination has been sent the “switch to alternate master key”
                            indication, TX will switch to alternate master key as the input for all further master
                            key derivations for that destination.
                         5. Once all destinations have been sent the “switch to alternate master key” indication,
                            TX will set an alternate master key switch timeout timer. Once this timeout timer is
                            set, TX will track which destinations have gone through a key derivation flow.
                                 •   Note that SW is free to program different values for key swap message
                                     timeout timer and alternate master key switch timeout timer.
                         6. Once the master key switch timeout time expires, TX shall trigger a key derivation
                            flow for all destinations for which a key derivation flow has not occurred. This
                            ensures that all destinations have switched to the alternate master key.
                         7. Once all destinations have started using a derived key that used the alternate master
                            key as input, the TX will inform the security processor in the accelerator that the key
                            swap flow is complete. The security processor in turn will inform the node CCM SW.
             This key swap flow is illustrated in Figure 8-8.

             8.5.7.5 RX implementation
             As mentioned in Section8.5.7.1, in an UALink port RX, there will be an active master key and an
Evaluation Copy
             alternate master key. The active key is fed into a NIST approved Key Derivation Function (KDF)
             defined in Section 8.5.7.2 to generate a derived key for each source accelerator. This is detailed in
             Section 8.5.7.2.1. This derived source specific key is then used along with a {per source accelerator,
             per stream} IV (format defined in section 8.5.7.3) to decrypt transactions received by the RX. New
             key derivation in the RX for each source accelerator is driven by the source accelerator TX. The key
             derivation flow is detailed in Section 8.5.7.2. The key derivation flow involves three
             “KeyDeriveMSG” request – response pairs – one for each stream from the source accelerator. When
             the RX receives a KeyDeriveMSG.ReqChannel request on the request stream, it shall switch to doing
             decryption using a new key derived from the active master key on the very first request received
             after the “KeyDeriveMSG.ReqChannel” request. For the read response channel, the RX shall switch
             to doing decryption using a new key derived from the active master key from the very first
             response received after the response for the “KeyDeriveMSG.RdRspChannel” request. For the write
             response channel, the RX shall switch to doing decryption using a new key derived from the active
             master key from the very first response received after the response for the
             “KeyDeriveMSG.WrRspChannel” request. These sequence of actions are illustrated in Figure 8-10.
             To achieve this instantaneous switch for a given source accelerator, RX is required to pre-compute
             and keep the derived key ready for the next epoch as detailed in Figure 8-9. The master key swap in
             RX for a particular source accelerator is triggered by the source accelerator TX. Since each source
             accelerator TX can request for a key swap independent of other source accelerators, at any given
             instant, the master key to be used in key derivation for some source accelerators could be the active
             key and the master key for the rest of the source accelerators could be alternate master key. The RX
             shall keep track of the master key to be used for each source accelerator. The IV counter shall be 0
             for the very first transaction decrypted by RX using a new derived key.




                                                            Security                                           219

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                    Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                        Rev 2.0

             8.5.7.5.2 Allowing backpressure
             RX is permitted to back pressure the remote TX if it is not able to complete key derivation in time
             for switching to the new derived key. In practice, this is expected to manifest as TX running out of
             credits since the crypto engine will not process incoming transactions till it has completed the key
             derivation. Note that this has performance implications and hence should be avoided unless
             necessary.
             8.5.7.6 SW forced key swap
             UALink Rx and Tx shall offer a secure interface to SW/FW to force key swap of a master key.
             To prevent the race condition when SW_forced_key swap is triggered just after an HW triggered key
             swap and before a new alternate Key has been installed, the UALink shall have a programmable
             watchdog counter to count the clock cycles after the last HW triggered key swap to allow the
             SW/FW enough time to program a new alternate Key.



                                                                                           Security                                                                            221

                           © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

             8.5.7.7 Invalidating Master Key and Error recovery
             There may be a need to invalidate the installed master keys either due to authentication failure on a
Evaluation Copy
             payload received or other reasons that require clearing the installed master keys.
             UALink Rx and Tx shall offer a secure interface to SW/FW to invalidate installed master keys
             (current key and alternate key if installed). Note: Traffic associated to with the master key to be
             invalidated must be quiesced before invalidation.
             After an authentication failure, to re-enable a connection between AcceleratorX/PortA and
             AcceleratorY/PortB the SW/FW shall follow the following procedure:
                  1) Quiesce the traffic between AcceleratorX/PortA and AcceleratorY/PortB
                  2) Invalidate installed master keys on AcceleratorX for Tx and Rx connections to
                     AcceleratorY/PortB
                  3) Invalidate installed master keys on AcceleratorY for Tx and Rx connections to
                     AcceleratorX/PortA
                  4) Install Master Key on AcceleratorX for Tx and Rx connections to AcceleratorY/PortB
                  5) Install Master Key on AcceleratorY for Tx and Rx connections to AcceleratorX/PortA
             Traffic between AcceleratorX/PortA and AcceleratorY/PortB can resume after the derived keys are
             generated.
             8.5.7.8 UALink Security resources mapping
             While a UALink system can have up to 1024 accelerators in a Pod, an individual UALink port may
             connect only to a smaller subset. Each UALink port has a set of “UALink security resource”
             instances. On devices with support for reduced storage, an “UALink_security resource” instance
             comprises of all the storage required for configuring and maintaining a secure connection to a
             target accelerator port:
             This includes:
                  •   Tx/Rx
                  •   1 Current Derived Key
                  •   1 Next Derived Key
                  •   3 IV values (1 for each stream)
                  •   1 Epoch counter
             Note: Current Master Key, the Alternate Master Key and the master key usage counter are common
             resources and state to the whole UALink device and are not replicated for each UALink_security
             resource.
             For each UALink port, the Virtual Pod Controller shall be responsible for assigning and configuring
             the required UALink security resources to the set of accelerators to which the port is supposed to
             be connected to. A HW-SW interface shall be provided by the UALink port HW to do the assignment
             and configuration. The number of UAlink security resources for each port are required to be
             discoverable by security SW.
             The UALink Security resources assigned to Rx (and Tx) shall have a consecutive UALink Security
             resources index.

             8.5.8 Initializing encrypted and authenticated transmission and reception
             For UALink 2.0, It is assumed that encryption (and optionally, authentication) is enabled on a per
             virtual Pod basis. This means that all traffic transmitted or received by a port belonging to that pod
                                                                       Security                                                       230

                     © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                       Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                           Rev 2.0

             will be encrypted and optionally authenticated. This also means that all ports on an accelerator are
             required to have the same setting for encryption enable and authentication enable. In addition, it is
Evaluation Copy
             assumed that no valid transactions are sent out by an UALink TX prior to configuring and enabling
             encryption and authentication. This ensures that the very first transaction that an RX receives is an
             encrypted (and optionally authenticated) transaction. With these assumptions, the trusted SW (i.e.,
             CCM within a TVM) is expected to do the following for initialization of encrypted (and optionally
             authenticated) transmission and reception:
                  1.   Generate active and alternate master keys for the virtual POD.
                  2.   Program the active and alternate master keys into each TX and RX port in the virtual POD.
                  3.   Program the derived key expiry threshold in each TX and RX. The same threshold is
                       programmed into all TX and RX.
                  4.   Program the master key usage limit in each TX. The same threshold is programmed into all
                       TX.
                  5.   Program the set of destination accelerator IDs in each TX port. This is used for IV formation
                       and as part of the input to KDF.
                  6.   Program the logical source port number in each TX port . This is used for IV formation and
                       as part of the input to KDF. TX is required to know its own accelerator ID and port number
                  7.   Program the set of {source accelerator ID, source port number} combinations in each RX
                       port. This is used for IV formation and as part of the input to KDF. RX is required to know its
                       own accelerator ID and port number
             An example flow for initialization is as follows:
             •    For each UALink TX in the pod:
                       o   The CCM in the lead node of the virtual POD instructs the CCM of the sub-ordinate node
                           which has the accelerator containing the UALink TX to do the security related
                           programming. The lead CCM also provides the master keys (active and alternate), the
                           master key usage limit and derived key expiry threshold
                       o   The sub-ordinate node CCM that manages the accelerator containing the UALink TX
                           does the following:
                              ▪   The CCM SW configures the active master key value and alternate master key
                                  value in each UALink TX in the accelerator. CCM configures the derived key
                                  expiry threshold and the master key usage limit in each UAlink TX in the
                                  accelerator.
                       o   Once these steps are completed, the sub-ordinate node CCM that manages the
                           accelerator containing the UALink TX informs the lead CCM that programming is
                           complete.
             •    For each UALink RX in the pod:
                       o   The CCM in the lead node of the virtual POD instructs the CCM of the sub-ordinate node
                           which has the accelerator containing the UALink RX to do the security related
                           programming. The lead CCM also provides the master keys (active and alternate), the
                           master key usage limit and derived key expiry threshold


                                                              Security                                            231

                        © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

                      o   The sub-ordinate node CCM that manages the accelerator containing the UALink TX
                          does the following:
Evaluation Copy
                             ▪   The CCM SW configures the active master key value and alternate master key
                                 value registers in each UALink RX in the accelerator .
             Note that transmission shall not start until key derivation is complete.
                      o   Once these steps are completed, the sub-ordinate node CCM that manages the
                          accelerator containing the UALink RX informs the lead CCM that programming is
                          complete.
             •    Once these steps are complete for all TX and RX pairs in the Virtual Pod, the lead CCM instructs
                  the sub-ordinate CCMs in the Virtual Pod of all accelerators in the pod to enable encrypted and
                  (optionally) authenticated transaction reception in all UALink ports.
             •    Once reception is enabled in all accelerators, the lead CCM instructs the sub-ordinate CCMs of
                  all accelerators in the pod to enable encrypted and (optionally) authenticated transaction
                  transmission in all UALink ports.
             •    The very first valid transaction that a RX receives will be encrypted and (optionally)
                  authenticated transaction.

             8.5.9 Refreshing an expired key
             The node CCM and/or lead node CCM is expected to trigger a key refresh. For this, CCM can
             potentially trigger key refresh periodically or it can monitor the bandwidth usage of various ports
             and then trigger the key refresh if the Bandwidth usage crosses a specific threshold.
             The following is an example flow for refreshing an expired key.
                  •   The lead node CCM key refresh timer expires. The lead node CCM generates a new key.
                  •   The lead node CCM will instruct all the node CCMs to trigger a key swap.
                  •   The node CCMs will trigger key swaps in all UALink TXes and wait for the completion of the
                      key swap to be reported from all UAlink TXes in the node
                  •   Once all node CCMs report to the lead node CCM that the key swap has been completed, the
                      lead node CCM supplies the new key to all the node CCMs to replace the key that has
                      become stale.
                  •   The node CCMs will program the new key into all the UALink TXes and RXes to replace the
                      stale key.
                  •   Once the node CCMs report to the Lead node CCM that the new key programming is
                      complete, lead node CCM will restart the key refresh timer.

             8.5.10 Safeguarding UALink configuration to ensure confidentiality and integrity
             From a security perspective, registers in the UALink port/station can be classified into two
             categories
                  A. Registers that only trusted SW/FW shall configure. E.g., the Active master key register. To
                     ensure confidentiality and integrity, such registers shall have restricted access such that
                     only SW/FW that are trusted can write to them.


                                                             Security                                          232

                       © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                       Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                           Rev 2.0

                  B. Registers (and configuration storage arrays/lookup tables) that should be configured by
                     untrusted SW/FW (e.g., address decoding related registers), but can cause loss of
Evaluation Copy
                     confidentiality and/or integrity and/or execution corruption if maliciously modified. These
                     registers shall have a “lock” mechanism which ensures that only secure software/firmware
                     has write access once the register/array is in locked state. This ensures that untrusted
                     SW/FW can configure these registers and then trusted SW/FW can lock the register
                     (preventing further updates by untrusted SW/FW), verify the configuration’s validity and
                     then enable secure transmission/reception. The “lock” shall be set by the DSM by writing to
                     one or more registers that only DSM can access.
             It is the implementer’s responsibility to conduct a security audit of all configuration registers (and
             configuration storage arrays/look up tables) and decide which registers need to be in category A
             and which needs to be in category B.

             8.5.11 Integrity failure handling
             As soon as an integrity check failure is detected for a transaction within a stream, the receiver shall
             drop the failing transaction and stop processing new transactions from that source (all streams). In
             addition, the receiver will report a security failure to the security processor within the accelerator
             via hardware/firmware means that cannot be manipulated by untrusted HW/SW/FW. The security
             processor within the accelerator is responsible for informing the TVM (essentially CCM) of the
             failure.
             There is no cross-port impact of authentication failures. For example, if port A on an accelerator
             receives a packet and it fails the authentication check, it does not impact the processing of incoming
             or outgoing traffic on other ports of the same accelerator. However, it is expected that the software
             will bring everything to stop since failure on one part of the Virtual Pod will likely have a cascading
             effect on the entire workload requiring a reset of the Virtual Pod. Software’s handling of the failure
             is outside the scope of this specification.

             8.5.12 Switch requirements
             Switches need to support the following:
                  1.   Detecting that the control flit has an associated tag half flit at ingress ports.
                  2.   Unpacking of tag half flits and associating each control half flit request/response with its tag
                  3.   Packing the tag of a transaction into a tag half flit at egress.
                  4.   Maintaining the order for each stream between a source accelerator port and destination
                       accelerator port. Note that the traffic between a SrcID, DstID pair would include
                       KeyRollMSG requests and corresponding responses.
             8.5.12.1 Virtual channel support with security enabled
             UALinkSec 2.0 specification supports link protection when virtual channel (VC) support is enabled
             in two modes: Strongly ordered [SO] and Strongly ordered lite [SOL].
             In Strongly ordered [SO] mode, the traffic for each stream must be kept in order from a given TX
             accelerator port to a given RX accelerator port regardless of VC on which the transaction is sent.
             In Strongly ordered Lite [SOL] mode, the traffic must kept in order for a given {stream, VC} pair –
             i.e. there is no ordering requirement between streams belonging to different VCs. i.e.,




                                                               Security                                            233

                        © 2025-2026 ULTRA ACCELERATOR LINK CONSORTIUM, INC. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.
                      Ultra Accelerator Link Consortium Inc. (UALink) - UALink Common Specification
                                                          Rev 2.0

             8.5.12.1.1 State replication requirements for SOL mode
             In a UALink network that supports SOL mode , each VC is expected to have the request stream, the
Evaluation Copy
             write response stream and the read response stream. While within a VC the traffic per stream is
             required to be in-order, traffic belonging to different VCs are completely unordered with respect to
             each other. This requires each VC to maintain its own stream related crypto resources.
             The crypto resources maintained per VC include:
                  •   Derived key (one per destination)
                  •   Epoch counter (one per destination)
                  •   Next derived key (one per destination)
                  •   IV (one per {destination, stream} pair)
             Derived key generation shall include VC ID as one of the inputs to the KDF as described in 1.5.7.2.
             IV construction will also include VC ID as one of the input to the KDF as described in 1.5.7.3.
             The key derivation and key swap flows shall be done by each TX for all {VC, destination} pairs.

             8.5.13 Datapath protection
             This section specifies requirement for data path protection of the AES-GCM engines. This feature is
             optional and can be enabled during UALink security configuration when Authentication is enabled.
             Data path protection provides robustness against hard and soft faults internal to the encryption and
             decryption engines. CRC-32 is calculated on the message Plaintext content (PCRC) . The encrypted
             PCRC (ECRC) integrates into the standard Auth_TAG check mechanism and does not consume any
             incremental link bandwidth. It can be implemented without adding significant incremental latency.
             A polynomial with the coefficients 1EDC 6F41h shall be used for PCRC calculation. PCRC calculation
             shall begin with the polynomial initialized to FFFF_FFFFh (CRC seed). The PCRC shall be calculated
             across all the bytes of plaintext messages that are part of the given Auth_TAG. PCRC calculation
             shall begin with bit0 byte0 of message plaintext content and sequentially include bits 0 - 7 for each
             byte of the message contents that are mapped to the plaintext. After accumulating the 32-bit value
             across the message contents, the PCRC value shall be finalized by taking 1’s complement of the bits
             of accumulated value to obtain PCRC[31:0].
             On the transmitter side, during the encryption flow, the PCRC value shall be appended to the end of
             the aggregated flit plaintext content, encrypted to form the Encrypted CRC (ECRC), and then
             included in the Auth_TAG calculation as shown in Figure 8-11.

                                                       Rev 2.0

                      Figure 8-11 PCRC generation and integration during encryption flow on the transmitter side

             On the receiver side, as shown in Figure 8-12, the PCRC value shall be recalculated based on the
             received, decrypted ciphertext message. When the last portion of the message of the current
             Auth_TAG has been processed, the accumulated PCRC value shall be XORed (encrypted) with the
             AES keystream bits that immediately follow the values that are used for decrypting the received
             cipher message. This encrypted PCRC value shall be appended to the end of the received ciphertext
             for the purposes of Auth_TAG computation.
